Prop 3: If a location is enclosed by a wall then the location is surrounded by the wall
Prop 4: If a person is shot from a direction then the bullet comes from that direction
Prop 5: If an object is disconnected from another object and a person is covered by the first object then the person is disconnected from the second object
Prop 6: If a room contains an object and the object covers another object then the room contains the second object
Prop 7: If a person is inside a location and the location is covered by an object then the person is inside the object
Prop 8: If a door is locked then a person cannot pass through the door
Prop 9: If a person is covered by an object and the object is disconnected from a location then the person is disconnected from the location
Prop 10: If an object is inside a room and the room is surrounded by a wall then the object is inside the wall

## OBJECTIVE:
**FIND THE CONTRADICTION:** Which evidence and testimony contradict each other?

## CONSTRAINT:
**You must only answer ONE PAIR.**

## ANALYSIS FRAMEWORK:
Please follow this format:

**Step 1:** **Analyze the spatial contradiction** between evidences and testimonies

**Step 2:** **Check if any spatial propositions** from the list above apply to this contradiction

**Step 3:** **If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

**Step 4:** **If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

**Step 5:** **Provide your answer in JSON format**

---

## CRITICAL OUTPUT REQUIREMENT:
**Your answer must end with a JSON format like so:** {"evidence": 2, "testimony": 3}

**The answer JSON should strictly be the last line, followed by nothing else.**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1:** Analyzing the spatial contradiction between evidences and testimonies, we notice that Testimony 3 states Ms. Crane was murdered, and with her dying breath, she managed to retaliate. However, Evidence 11, the Autopsy Report, indicates that the head injury was sustained post-mortem, meaning the retaliation, if it involved a physical act, could not have occurred with her "dying breath" as she would have been dead before the head injury.

**Step 2:** Checking if any spatial propositions apply to this contradiction, we see that Prop 4 (If a person is shot from a direction then the bullet comes from that direction) does not directly apply since the autopsy report does not mention a bullet wound but rather stab wounds and a head injury. However, the concept of timing and sequence of events (implied by the autopsy report) can be related to spatial propositions in a broader sense, considering the spatial arrangement of events in time.

**Step 3:** While none of the propositions directly address the contradiction between the testimony and the evidence regarding the timing of the retaliation and the cause of death, Prop 4 hints at the importance of understanding the direction or source of an action (in this case, the retaliation). Yet, it's more about the sequence and possibility of actions given the state of the individual (dead or alive) that reveals the contradiction.

**Step 4:** The spatial reasoning used here involves understanding the sequence of events and the physical state of the individual at different times. The testimony implies an action (retaliation) at the moment of death, while the evidence (autopsy report) suggests that at least one of the injuries (the head injury) occurred after death, making the described retaliation impossible if it required the victim to be alive.

**Step 5:** Given the analysis, the contradiction lies between the implication of Testimony 3 and the facts presented in Evidence 11.

{"evidence": 11, "testimony": 3}

--------------------
{
  "evidence": 11,
  "testimony": 3
}

However, we can identify a contradiction between the testimony describing the shot hitting the victim "square in the forehead" and the autopsy report indicating the bullet hit the right temple. This discrepancy does not directly relate to the spatial propositions provided but indicates a contradiction between the testimony and the evidence.

**Step 5:** Provide your answer in JSON format
Given the analysis, the contradiction identified is between Testimony 4 and Evidence 11, based on the description of where the bullet hit the victim.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1:** Analyze the spatial contradiction between evidences and testimonies. 
From the given information, we can see that there are several testimonies and evidences that describe the events and setting of the crime scene. We need to find a pair that contradicts each other.

**Step 2:** Check if any spatial propositions from the list above apply to this contradiction. 
Proposition 3 states that if a person is hit from the front, then the person falls backward. This proposition can be applied to the crime scene.

**Step 3:** If applicable propositions exist, explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction. 
Testimony 5 states that the defendant grabs the bottle and (implied to hit the victim), and Evidence 2 shows the victim slumped in a chair, leaning back, with a hat covering his eyes and forehead. However, the autopsy report (Evidence 1) states the death was caused by a single blow to the forehead, which implies the victim was hit from the front. According to Proposition 3, if the victim was hit from the front, he should fall backward. But Testimony 3 states that the victim was winning, implying he was still upright and engaged in the game until the end. The contradiction arises when considering the position of the victim's body in relation to the direction of the hit.

**Step 4:** If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction. 
However, since we found a applicable proposition, this step is not necessary.

**Step 5:** Provide your answer in JSON format. 
Given the analysis, the contradiction is between the direction of the hit (implied by the autopsy report and the position of the body) and the expected outcome of such a hit (as per Proposition 3). However, the direct contradiction in terms of spatial arrangement and testimony is more directly related to the testimonies about who was winning and the final actions, but the critical spatial contradiction involves the interpretation of the hit's direction and its implications on the body's position. The most relevant contradiction considering spatial propositions and their direct application to the provided evidence and testimonies involves the interpretation of events leading to the victim's death and the positioning of individuals and objects at the crime scene.

Prop 9: If a weapon is shot upward from a low location then the trajectory goes from the low location to a high location
Prop 10: If a person is in the same room as an object and there are no obstacles between them then the person can see the object

**Step 2:** Check if any spatial propositions from the list above apply to this contradiction

However, the more directly relevant proposition is Prop 1, which states that if a person is shot from a location, then the bullet comes from that location. This helps in understanding the direction from which the victim was shot.

Additionally, Prop 3 indirectly supports the analysis by implying that an upward shot would have a trajectory from low to high, which does not match the described wound trajectory.

**Step 4:** If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction
The spatial reasoning here involves understanding the trajectory of the bullet based on the entry and exit wounds. Given that the bullet entered the lower right abdomen and exited the right shoulder, it suggests a horizontal or slightly downward trajectory rather than an upward one, contradicting the testimony of being shot from on high.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1:** Analyzing the spatial contradiction between evidences and testimonies, we notice that Det. Gumshoe's Testimony (Testimony 2 and Testimony 3) states he was in the hallway the whole time until he heard the gunshot and didn't take a single step away from the Lobby No. 2 door. However, Evidence 11 (Det. Gumshoe's Fingerprints) and Evidence 9 (Swiss Roll) suggest that Det. Gumshoe might have moved from his position to eat a Swiss roll, as his fingerprints were found on the hallway bench with a scent of sweet sugar, and he bought a Swiss roll with Kay.

**Step 2:** Checking if any spatial propositions apply to this contradiction, we find that Prop 5 (If a location is disconnected from another location, then a person in the first location is disconnected from the second location) and Prop 7 (If a person is inside a room and that room is behind another room, then the person is behind the other room) do not directly apply because they deal with disconnection and relative positioning of rooms, not the movement of a person within a hallway or room.

**Step 3:** However, Prop 3 (If a person is in the same room as an object, then that person can potentially see the object) and Prop 8 (If a person is facing a direction, then that person can see what is in that direction) imply that if Det. Gumshoe was facing the Lobby No. 2 door, he should have seen anyone approaching or any significant events happening in front of him. But the critical aspect here is the potential contradiction between his testimony of not moving and the evidence suggesting he did move to eat a Swiss roll.

**Step 4:** The spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction involves understanding that if Det. Gumshoe was continuously in front of Lobby No. 2 as he claimed, it would be difficult for him to have eaten a Swiss roll without moving from his post, unless the bench where his fingerprints were found was directly in front of Lobby No. 2 and within his sightline. However, the testimonies and evidences provided do not specify the exact layout or the location of the bench relative to Det. Gumshoe's position. The contradiction arises from the implication that Det. Gumshoe must have moved to interact with Kay (buying a Swiss roll) and to leave his fingerprints on a bench, which contradicts his claim of not taking a single step away from the Lobby No. 2 door.

**Step 5:** Given the analysis, the contradiction is between Det. Gumshoe's testimony of not moving from his post (Testimony 3) and the evidence suggesting he did move (Evidence 11, Det. Gumshoe's Fingerprints, and indirectly Evidence 9, Swiss Roll). This indicates a potential inconsistency in Det. Gumshoe's alibi.
